# Agent Acceleration — Consolidated, Cross-Validated Research Dossier

**v3 — 2026-09-27: six §8.3 follow-up investigations merged (E94–E211, D95–D140); no new research**

Compiled 26 Sept 2026 (v1); v2 same day merges the Project's final document — Claude's P3 landscape survey "Agents get faster by calling models less" (doc 15, tagged **[C15]**) — by delta: its new works and numbers are added to the sections below, its conflicts with the other 15 documents are logged as D41–D55 in Part VI, and the provenance matrix, scorecard and verification queue are updated. This document merges every finding from the 14 uploaded reports plus Claude's own "too slow / too expensive" report and the Project survey, cross-checks them against each other, resolves the highest-impact conflicts against primary sources (five sources were re-fetched on 26 Sept 2026, marked **[V]**), and records what remains open. Nothing was intentionally dropped; where several providers said the same thing, it appears once with all attributions. Where they disagreed, both versions and the resolution appear.

v2.1 (27 Sept 2026) applies the verification pass on the §8.2 queue (P0 items 1–7, P1 items 8–13f) as patches, with no new research; the apply log is research/2026-09-27-v2.1-apply-log.md. What changed: D7 is resolved (OSWorld-Human Table 3 totals give $7.87 per task, calc.; the paper's $2.43 matches output-token cost only, inferred); D48 is overturned (FocusAgent's two WebArena results come from two retriever configurations, GPT-4.1-mini and GPT-5-mini, not from a sign flip between versions); Continuum (">8×" and 1.12–3.66× are both in v7, so D14 was not version drift), TraceLab ("5.3×" is not in the paper, which gives 3.8×), AXIS (its −65–70% task time is against manual human operation; against a UI agent it is ≈2×), ComputerRL ("≤1/3 the steps" is an unquantified author statement) and Gartner (the >90% is providers' inference cost, not customers' price; E73 rescoped to AI use cases in I&O) are corrected; E91–E93 (EchoPath, AWO) and D56–D94 are added; Top-10 ranks 6, 9 and 10 are reworded, and no Top-10 number changes value.

v3 (27 Sept 2026) merges the six §8.3 follow-up investigations (research/2026-09-27-followup-1-… to -6-…) as patches, with no new research; the merge log with the ID mapping and checks is research/2026-09-27-v3-merge-log.md. Per follow-up, rows added and the most important finding: (1) latency decomposition: 19 E rows (E94–E112) and 7 D rows (D95–D101); the harness, not the regime, decides the dominant latency term: a single-call OSWorld agent spends >70% of its time in the desktop sandbox (E97), Browser-Use spends 73% on LLM calls (E100), and Claude Code/Codex requests spend 59.8% in tool execution (E105); no breakdown in any regime reports a TTFT or prefill/decode split for a frontier API model with a measured cache hit rate. (2) speculation safety: 26 E rows (E113–E138) and 8 D rows (D102–D109); gating before release beats compensation after the fact (Atomix: Saga-style compensation leaked 400/500 irreversible effects vs 0/500 for gated commit, E113), and no paper evaluates speculation against a live, proprietary SaaS back end. (3) RPA + LLM fallback: 22 E rows (E139–E160) and 6 D rows (D110–D115); replay-then-heal ships at UiPath, Power Automate, Automation Anywhere, Stagehand, Skyvern and Hyperbrowser, but no vendor publishes a measured heal/replay success rate, heal latency or cost per successful run (E160); LOOP's "99% success" is a model, not a measurement (E140). (4) human-time baselines: 11 E rows (E161–E171) and 6 D rows (D116–D121); no standard computer-use/web benchmark pairs agent and human wall-clock on the same tasks; the cleanest same-task pair found (AXIS user study) has a 2024 UI agent 1.69× slower than manual on easy tasks and 0.93× on hard ones, calc. (E161). (5) 2026 agent-serving papers: 18 E rows (E172–E189) and 9 D rows (D122–D130); none of the 15 2026 agent-serving systems checked evaluates a GUI, computer-use, browser, OSWorld or WebArena workload, and only 3 of 15 report a task-quality delta. (6) vendor speed tiers: 22 E rows (E190–E211) and 7 D rows (D131–D137); Artificial Analysis "Time per Task" is decode time only, not end-to-end task time (E207); vendor speed tiers now include OpenAI Ultrafast (up to 14×, vendor-stated, unpriced preview; E194), and no independent measurement of any fast tier was found (E209). Three further D rows (D138–D140) record places where two follow-ups patched the same text and disagree; both texts are kept.

Please correct if any mapping is wrong.

Coverage: 16 documents. Every prompt now has at least one run from each provider except P5 (no ChatGPT run). ChatGPT contributed two P3 runs whose numbers differ in places (D15); Claude's P3 run (doc 15) was merged in v2 and its conflicts are D41–D55. Doc 15's own verification notes say it did **not** check OSWorld 2.0 or TheAgentCompany, so it contributes nothing to Part I and is used mainly for Parts III and V.

### 0.2 Labels used throughout

- **[C] / [O] / [G]** — which provider(s) reported the item. **[C15]** — Claude's P3 landscape survey from the Project (doc 15), merged in v2. **[V]** — re-verified by Claude on 26 Sept 2026 against the primary source.
- **✅** cross-validated (≥2 providers independently, or verified against the primary). **⚠** single-source, secondary, vendor-marketing, or version-sensitive. **❌** error or unverifiable claim found in one of the inputs.
- **Primary** = paper, benchmark leaderboard, vendor blog/docs, official survey report. **Secondary** = press, blog summaries, trackers.
- **Calc.** = arithmetic performed by a provider or by Claude in this dossier, not stated in the source.

### 0.3 Measurement-definition rules (ChatGPT's discipline, adopted here)

These distinctions decide whether a number supports the thesis. They are applied to every entry below.

1. **Model calls ≠ tool calls ≠ environment steps ≠ turns.** OSWorld 2.0 reports tool calls and steps separately (batched tools make several calls per step). GTA1 makes 4–12 planning calls per judging call. TheAgentCompany defines "steps" as LLM calls.
2. **Model-inference time ≠ end-to-end wall-clock.** The widely quoted "225–330 s per task" product comparison is pure LLM inference time per trajectory, not task time (verified, D1).
3. **Cost per attempted task ≠ cost per successful task.** $72.4/attempt at 20.6% completion ≈ $351 per observed success (calc.), but that is an accounting ratio, not the price of retrying one task until it works.
4. **Human "estimates" ≠ stopwatch trials.** OSWorld-Human's "<30 s" is the authors' estimate; OSWorld 2.0's "1.6 h" is an annotator-recorded estimate from people who performed the task (verified, D2).
5. **Version matters.** Several headline numbers changed between arXiv versions (OSWorld-Human step ratio, PASTE, Continuum, FocusAgent, Efficient Agents). Cite the version.

---

## 0.4 Executive synthesis (what the 15 reports jointly establish)

1. **Where the time goes depends on the agent architecture — there are three regimes, not one bottleneck.** Screenshot-based multi-call desktop agents without effective caching (OSWorld-Human) spend 87–97% of wall-clock in LLM planning/judging/reflection and are prefill-bound, with later steps up to 3× slower. Text/AXTree single-call web agents (BrowserGym, Skim) spend 50–62% of step time in the browser. Cached coding agents (TraceLab, Agentic AI Workload Characteristics (2605.26297)) see 85–99.5% cache hits, decode-dominated LLM time, and a heavy tool-latency tail (4% of tool calls = 85% of tool time). All three regimes appear across the inputs; only ChatGPT's P1 report noted the tension explicitly. Details in §1.8.
2. **The marginal cost of accuracy is steeply convex at the frontier.** OSWorld 2.0: 13.0%→20.6% completion costs ~6× output tokens (37.1K→224K/task; ~$25.5→$72.4/task), "roughly 25 to 30K extra tokens for each additional point" [V]. HAL: 9× cost for 2 points on Online-Mind2Web. Agentic Test-Time Scaling for WebAgents: 9.6× tokens for +4.4 points with the model held fixed. Three independent measurements, one shape.
3. **For interactive deployment, turns are the cost that matters, not tokens.** OSWorld 2.0 states this explicitly and shows the token-ranking inverts in turn space (Opus 4.8: most tokens, fewest turns via batching) [V]. Its protocol also inserts a 3-second pause after every action, so a 150–600-tool-call trajectory carries 7–30 minutes of forced waiting before any inference time (calc.).
4. **Fewer model calls does not imply faster.** AWO/meta-tools (AppWorld, Claude 4.5): calls fall 16.7→15.4 per task but time rises 75.6→88.1 s and success drops 89.3→85.7% (with GPT 5.1 the same method cuts calls 21.8→19.2 and time 266.6→200.8 s); FocusAgent's retriever cuts tokens but raises per-step model latency 2.5→10.1 s; DSP cuts latency 37% but raises cost 63%; Interactive Speculative Planning cuts time 42% but raises cost 38%.
5. **"Lossless" has three distinct senses** (by-construction trajectory preservation; statistical non-inferiority; no observed degradation), and no surveyed acceleration paper runs a formal non-inferiority test. Only exact-match, read-only speculation (Speculative Actions, Speculate-with-Memory, PASTE audit) is lossless by construction.
6. **Demand is exploding and is now agent-dominated on the largest public router.** OpenRouter: agentic tokens overtook human tokens "right around February 1st" 2026; agentic requests use ~15× the tokens of human requests; 450T+ tokens analyzed Jan 1–Jun 14 2026 [V]; agentic tokens grew 14× since ~6 Feb 2026 vs 2.8× for human, but ~70–85% of agentic tokens are cached prompt tokens (secondary reports of OpenRouter charts; D71). Gartner (primary, 25 Mar 2026): agentic models need 5–30× tokens per task vs a chatbot, and "overall inference costs are expected to increase" even as providers' cost of inference on a 1T-parameter LLM falls >90% (2025→2030), savings "not fully passed on to enterprise customers" [V]; Gartner (17 Aug 2026): inference cost per agentic workflow to rise more than fivefold through 2028 [V].
7. **Survey evidence supports "latency constrains interaction design and which tasks get deployed," not "latency blocks deployment."** LangChain: latency is the #2 production barrier (20%, N=1,340). MAP (ICML 2026 oral): only 14.8% of deployed survey agents report latency as a critical deployment blocker (small N, ≈27); 15 of 20 interviewed systems can operate asynchronously. Temporal: 79.8% say cost is at least somewhat a factor in their decision to use agents, yet 91.1% report productivity gains.
8. **Vendors are monetizing speed.** Anthropic and OpenAI both sell "fast mode" at up to 2.5× output speed for 2× price; Microsoft's priority tier sets 50–100 tokens/s targets and downgrades >128k-token prompts on older models (gpt-5.4, gpt-4.1); OpenAI previews a 14× "Ultrafast" tier on Cerebras (unpriced) and Google charges 75–100% more for Priority; Google moved computer use into Flash/Flash-Lite; Anthropic switched Claude in Chrome's default to Haiku 4.5 for responsiveness.
9. **Landscape (8 families):** the largest measured gains come from eliminating calls outright (compiled GUI programs: Agent JIT 9.7×, AutoDroid-V2 −93% inference time, GPA 329→34 s), exact KV reuse and program-level scheduling in serving (SGLang 6.4×, Autellix/Agentix 4–15×, Continuum 1.1–3.7× delay (trace replay; up to 8.18× on a real SWE-agent run)), and parallel tool dispatch (LLMCompiler 3.74×). Lossless action speculation delivers ~20–45% (and Speculative Actions proves a **50% cap for single-step speculation** [C15]). Observation compression and routing help but usually trade accuracy. Doc 15 sharpens this into a ranking: **call-count reducers (executable skills, site-derived tools, native APIs) are the one family that is both large and accuracy-positive** (ASI +23.5% rel., WALT 50.1% WebArena, ComputerRL +134% rel. success for API–GUI vs GUI-only [its "≤1/3 the steps" is an unquantified author statement], AXIS ≈2× faster than a UI agent with 3.2→2.0 steps and −65–70% task time vs manual human operation), whereas text-only workflow memory does not reliably save steps (in ASI's controlled re-run AWM used 5.9 steps vs 5.6 for vanilla). Practitioner stacking order: lossless serving/caching/parallelism first, call-count reducers second, lossy per-step levers (routing, pruning, lower effort) last.
10. **Evaluation practice is weak:** single runs per configuration (HAL, Efficient Agents, Agentless), undated prices, emulated or inference-only latency, and "comparable accuracy" claims without margins. A first paper can differentiate by pairing a construction guarantee with a pre-registered paired non-inferiority test and by reporting wall-clock, calls, tokens, dollars and cost-of-pass together.
11. **Novelty for the SAP Concur plan:** every component exists (skill/recipe compilation, speculative actions, plan caching, pruning, routing, cost-Pareto evaluation), and five of the ten closest works are Apr–Sep 2026 preprints. What is still open: speculation over irreversible, non-snapshotable UI transitions with commit/compensation semantics; profile-guided recipe compilation with guarded de-optimization learned from own runs plus a few demos; and field-level, per-lever accuracy-loss attribution with three-way accuracy–latency–cost frontiers over repeated runs.
12. **Data hygiene:** several widely repeated numbers in the inputs are wrong, misattributed, or mis-defined (Part VI lists 140). The most consequential: the 225–330 s "task time" (inference time), "318 median tool calls" (mean, single-action Opus 4.7), Efficient Agents "$0.228/28.4%" (repeated by G9, G10 and C15), APC "0% degradation", the "MacroPO" paper (an AI-generated idea file), Gemini's attribution of OSWorld 2.0's marginal-token finding to an HPCA paper, and — added in v2 — FocusAgent's WebArena result, whose sign depends on the retriever (−4.2 pp with GPT-4.1-mini, unchanged since v1; +3.1 pp with GPT-5-mini, added in the TMLR revision), and which the inputs misreported as a sign flip between versions (D48), plus doc 15's claim that no controlled multi-action study exists on OSWorld, which UFO2 Table 7 and OSWorld 2.0 Table 3 contradict (D46).

**Reading.** Same-task evidence points in both directions. GUI agents are slower than people on short GUI tasks (AXIS L1, CADWorld) and roughly equal on harder ones (AXIS L2). Agents are much faster on long artefact or search tasks, at lower quality (FrontierFinance, Mind2Web 2, GDPval) (D121). Expert *estimates* miss real time in both directions: +24% (APEX-Agents, calc.) and −32% (FrontierFinance, calc.).

| E120 | **Cordon semantics (mechanism)**: local mutations run speculatively in shadow state (rollback semantics); external effects wait in an outbox with idempotency keys (commit semantics); no compensation of released external effects | Design | same, §3–§4, Limitations | v1 | Primary preprint | followup-2 | High. For a GUI portal every click that submits a form is an "outward-facing" effect. Cordon's substrate can hold such an effect (defer) but cannot undo it once released → D105 Quote: "Local mutations have rollback semantics because the transaction can restore their pre-transaction anchors. External effects have commit semantics because their release may cross an irreversible boundary."; "Reversible local mutations execute speculatively in shadow state, while outward-facing actions are staged in an effect outbox"; "Dispatched effects are not resent unless the runtime has idempotency evidence that the previous dispatch did not complete." Limitation: "Operations that cross external or opaque boundaries, such as unsupported plugins, dynamically changing services, or tools with unobservable side effects, may fall outside this containment scope." |

Not previously in the dossier Quote: "Actions resulting in GET requests are typically non-destructive, as they merely retrieve data. | E172 | **CacheScout: KV-cache hit rate +10–18 pp; mean TTFT −18–45%; mean per-turn latency −29–38%; peak throughput up to +57%; on larger models TTFT up to −54% with 37% higher throughput** | Hit rate = cached prompt tokens / total prompt tokens. A counterfactual upper bound computed by the dashboard, not a measured deployment | https://tracelab.cs.washington.edu | dashboard, undated (data through 24 Jul 2026); accessed 2026-09-27 | primary (authors' dashboard; not in the paper behind E19) | followup-5 | Medium: a live page that may change. The only real-trace estimate in scope of what a Continuum-style TTL policy could save when the idle gap is **human** think time. Coding agents, not GUI. Extends E19 (TraceLab) Quote: *"Append reduction 2.70B \| Cost reduction $14,333 \| Final cost saved 15.8%"*; *"Upper-bound savings if user-initiated steps kept their prefix cache."* (section "Cost of human thinking time") |

Virginia Tech / Cisco / U. Minnesota. Pins down the "~30%" duplicate-request prior that AgentReuse cites (§3.2). It concerns chat queries, not agent steps; an approximate semantic cache Quote: §III-C: *"about 31% of user queries were similar to previous ones"*. Code: *"https://github.com/SEED-VT/MeanCache"* |

| E210 | **Claude in Chrome: Haiku 4.5 default (15 Oct 2025) is no longer visible on the cited page; "Quick Mode" experiment reported ~Feb 2026 (Anthropic staff posts on X) claiming ~3× faster; current help docs name no default model** | Product record / documentation review | https://support.claude.com/en/articles/12138966-release-notes (read 27 Sep 2026: earliest visible entry 16 Jan 2026); https://support.claude.com/en/articles/12012173-get-started-with-claude-in-chrome ; X posts https://x.com/gabemulley/status/2026041548429828101 and https://x.com/trq212/status/2026110215561822374 (robots-blocked; text from search snippets); secondary https://pasqualepillitteri.it/en/news/346/claude-chrome-quick-mode-fast-browsing-guide (HTTP 502) | 15 Oct 2025 (Haiku default); X post IDs decode to 23–24 Feb 2026 (calc., snowflake timestamp) | Primary docs (current state); Quick Mode **secondary / snippet only** | followup-6 | ⚠ E80's wording stays correct for Oct 2025, but its URL no longer shows the entry (D136). Quick Mode is a harness-level speed move (fewer and shorter action tokens), not a model-tier move. Unverified until a primary Anthropic page is found. It says nothing about speed tiers or agentic share (tool found no mention). Belongs in §1.5 rather than §1.7. ## 1.8 Synthesis: where the time actually goes — three regimes

The single most useful cross-provider finding is that the inputs contain three mutually inconsistent latency profiles that are all correct for their own agent class:

| Regime | Representative measurements | Architecture | Dominant term | Implication for acceleration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Screenshot-based desktop agents (caching state not reported)** | OSWorld-Human: 87–97% of time in planning/judging/reflection for multi-call o3/GPT-4.1 scaffolds; screenshots + actions <3.5%; later steps up to 3× slower; 4–12 planning calls per judging call (E22–E25). UFO2: LLM ≈10 s per inference dominates every configuration, OmniParser +≈1 s (E94). AXTree generation 3–26 s per observation (E24). Counter-case: single-call Kimi-K2.6 GUIAgent on OSWorld spends >70% in the desktop sandbox (E97); OSWorld's harness pauses 2 s per step by default (E111) and OSWorld 2.0 mandates 3 s (E6). Anthropic: 1,000–1,800 tokens per screenshot (E31). User's Concur profile: 71% of wall-clock waiting on the model, 57% of calls page-determined | Planner + judge/reflection over full screenshot history (multi-call), or one call per step over screenshot ± UIA/AXTree | **LLM prefill of a growing multimodal context and the number of sequential model calls — for multi-call scaffolds.** For single-call agents, environment waits (fixed pauses, AXTree generation) can dominate (D95) | Model-side levers dominate for multi-call scaffolds: eliminate calls (compiled recipes), batch actions per turn, cache prefixes, prune screenshot history, speculate observations. For single-call agents also cut fixed pauses and observation generation (event-driven readiness instead of sleeps) |
| **B. Text/AXTree/DOM single-call web agents** | Skim: median 4.7 s LLM vs 6.6 s browser per step (E17, GPT-4o, live sites); BrowserGym: 7.6 of 12.2 s per step in the browser (E26, not re-verified); AgentSysBench WebAgent: browser dominates with one observation format, but LLM rises to 61.6% with AXTree+HTML+screenshot (E98); Agent JIT: Browser-Use spends 73% of latency on LLM calls (E100); "What Limits…": web fetch up to 53.7% for a text QA agent (E27, misfit); PASTE: tools 45–57% (E28) | One decision call per step over a pruned DOM/AXTree | **Browser/network for compact observations and fast models on live sites; the LLM for rich observations, slower models, or self-hosted sites (E98, E100).** Provider queueing inside the "LLM" term can vary up to 69.21× (E101) | Environment-side levers (prefetch/pre-navigate, URL synthesis, parallel tool dispatch, speculative page loads) when browser-bound; call elimination (Agent JIT) and observation pruning when LLM-bound. Whether faster inference alone yields <50% depends on the measured split |
| **C. Cached long-session coding agents** | TraceLab: 95.7% prefix-hit rate, yet prefix tokens 59.5% of cost; per request tools 59.8% vs LLM 41.0% of time; per session human thinking 92.3% (E19, E105). Copilot: user idle 80.1%; LLM 13.7% vs tools 2%; tool median 166 ms (E106). Agentic AI Workload Characteristics: tools 2–29% of runtime, decode 91.0–98.6% of LLM time in a no-thrashing regime (E29). ThunderAgent: KV thrashing raises request latency up to 7.14× (E108). AgentSysBench Claude Code: LLM up to 90% or MCP tools up to 84% by task family (E99) | Append-only context with tool interleaving | **Human think time per session; within agent time, decode when KV stays resident, or the tool-latency tail when tools are heavy (tests, builds); re-prefill when KV is evicted under load** (D99, D100) | Serving-side levers: KV retention across tool waits (Continuum, KVFlow, CacheScout, ThunderAgent), program-level scheduling, avoiding context edits that break the prefix; tool-side levers (speculative/parallel tool execution, PASTE) where tools dominate |

Consequences for the thesis and for the user's paper:
- "Agents are too slow because inference is slow" is true for multi-call Regime A scaffolds, conditional in B (true for rich observations or slow models, e.g. Browser-Use 73% LLM; false for compact observations on live sites, e.g. Skim 4.7 s vs 6.6 s), and in C true only for agent time with light tools (Copilot) — not for heavy-tool sessions (TraceLab tools 59.8%) or for session wall-clock, which is 80–92% human think time. Any slide must state the regime **and** the harness (number of calls per step, observation format, fixed pauses, cache state) behind a number (followup-1, D95, D96, D99).
- OSWorld 2.0's turn-space result (batched Opus 4.8 reaches the best score in ~103 turns; single-action Sonnet 4.6/MiniMax need 250–325 turns for <10%) [V] means that **reducing environment turns is a first-class objective**, independent of tokens — this is exactly what compiled recipes and action batching attack.
- The forced 3-s pause per action in OSWorld 2.0's protocol, and OSWorld 2.0's "stale interface state" failure mode, show that latency is also an **accuracy** problem on dynamic UIs, not only a cost problem.
- No published breakdown in any regime reports a TTFT or prefill/decode split for a frontier API model with a measured cache hit rate. The only prefill/decode split (E29) is for local 27–31B models on vLLM, and no Regime A breakdown reports a cache hit rate. This gap is added to §8.1 candidates (followup-1).

## 1.9 The most persuasive numbers for a senior ML professor (merged and ranked)

Each provider proposed five; merged, de-duplicated and ranked by (evidence quality × relevance × freshness). Confidence tags apply.

| Rank | Number | Why it persuades | Caveat to state | Backing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **87–97% of computer-use-agent wall-clock time is LLM planning/judging/reflection; screenshots + actions <3.5%** (OSWorld-Human Table 1, MLSys 2026) | A measured, component-level profile that locates the bottleneck in model inference for screenshot agents | Regime A only (§1.8); 39-task profile | ✅ C, O |
| 2 | **13.0% → 20.6% completion on OSWorld 2.0 costs ~6× output tokens (37.1K → 224K/task; ~$25.5 → ~$72.4), "roughly 25 to 30K extra tokens for each additional point"; the best agent still fails ~4 of 5 human-scale (median 1.6 h) tasks** | Author-stated convex cost–accuracy frontier on realistic tasks, plus a 20.6% ceiling | Cross-model comparison, not a within-model law; output tokens only; human time is an annotator estimate | ✅ [V] C, O, G |
| 3 | **9.6× more tokens for +4.4 points with the model held fixed (WebArena-Lite; 96K → 920K tokens/task; marginal cost per point rises 5.7×)** | The only controlled within-model curve; removes the "different model" confound | Uniform sampling baseline; the paper's own adaptive method does better | ✅ single primary (O) |
| 4 | **9× cost for a 2-point difference on HAL's Online-Mind2Web ($171 vs $1,577 per 300 tasks); Opus 4.1 skipped because it would cost ~$20,000; researchers spent ~$40,000 for one run per configuration** | Independent third-party harness; shows the frontier is too expensive even to evaluate | Single runs; 2025 prices; no caching | ✅ C, O |
| 5 | **Each later step can take up to 3× longer than early steps because every prompt re-sends the screenshot history (prefill-bound)** | A mechanism (context replay) that a systems audience recognizes, predicting worse scaling on longer tasks | Caching changes the constant; Regime C is decode-bound | ✅ C, G, O |
| 6 | **Agentic tokens overtook human tokens on OpenRouter ~1 Feb 2026; agentic requests use ≈15× the tokens of human requests; agentic volume grew 14× in ~6 months (human 2.8×)** | Shows the efficiency problem is now the majority workload | One router; "Mixed" category; 70–85% of agentic tokens cached (secondary reports disagree, D71); 14×/2.8× read only via secondary reports of a LinkedIn post; "≈15×" is per request, not aggregate volume (≈5×, D70); not first-party API data | ✅ [V] O, G (Feb 1, 15×); ⚠ 14×/2.8× secondary-only (checked 2026-09-27) |
| 7 | **12 minutes (agent) vs <30 seconds (novice human) to double-space two paragraphs** | Concrete, falsifiable ~24× gap on a trivial task | Human side is an estimate; 2025 models | ✅ C, G, O |
| 8 | **318 tool calls per task (Opus 4.7, single-action) on tasks a skilled human does in a median 1.6 h; 20–24% completion under 45 min, 0% above 163 min** | Scale of iteration on human-scale work, plus horizon collapse | "318" is one configuration; batched runs use fewer steps but more calls | ✅ [V] C, G, O |
| 9 | **Latency is the #2 production barrier (20% of 1,340 practitioners) — but only 14.8% of deployed teams call it a critical blocker and 15 of 20 interviewed systems can operate asynchronously** | Connects the systems problem to deployment while pre-empting the obvious rebuttal | Self-selected samples; the MAP blocker question has N≈27 (caption "N=27–29"); "can operate" asynchronously, only 5/20 are background async processes | ✅ C, O; verified 2026-09-27 |
| 10 | **Gartner (primary): agentic models need 5–30× tokens per task vs a chatbot, and overall inference cost is expected to rise even as providers' cost of inference on a 1T-parameter LLM falls >90% (2025→2030) — savings "not fully passed on to enterprise customers" (25 Mar 2026); inference cost per agentic workflow to rise more than fivefold through 2028 (17 Aug 2026)** | Analyst confirmation that per-token deflation does not solve per-task cost | Analyst forecasts without a disclosed sample, method or baseline year; the 90% is provider cost, not customer price | ✅ [V] O (primary), C; both releases verified 2026-09-27 |

Numbers **not** to put on a slide without heavy caveats: NVIDIA's 100× / 1,000% / 1,000,000× (E64); "88% of OpenRouter tokens are agentic" (E59); "225–330 s per task" as wall-clock (E36); OSWorld-Human's $2.43/task (E45/D7: matches output-token cost only; use $7.87/task, calc. from Table 3 totals); Efficient Agents' "$0.228 / 28.4%" (E48); the 62–136× GPU-energy multiplier without its conditions (E49: verified, but HotpotQA only, one request served alone, maximum-accuracy Reflexion/LATS, Llama-3.1 on A100-40GB, vs one ShareGPT inference); any Browser Use cost-per-solved-task figure (E47); Agentic Compilation's "1500×" (a modeled projection over proprietary DOMs, §3.1); AgentReuse's "−93% latency" (near-duplicate workloads, §3.2); Fara-7B's "36×" (list-price ratio; independent run 62% vs claimed 73.5%, E90).

Honourable mention for a coding-agent audience: **93.2% for $2.45 (retry baseline) vs 88.0% for $134.50 (LATS)** on HumanEval (E89) — the cleanest single number showing that agent complexity, not model capability, drives cost.

---

# PART II — Prompt 2: Mapping LLM inference optimizations onto agent workflows

## 2.1 Framing (what all three providers agree on)

At the inference level the optimization target is one request: TTFT (prefill-bound), TPOT (decode-bound), throughput per GPU. Autellix states the mismatch directly: request-level metrics overlook end-to-end latency for agentic programs [C]. At the agent level the unit is a **step** (one model call plus one or more tool/environment actions), repeated until termination, so

Cost(task) ≈ Σ_steps [ prefill(context_t) + decode(output_t) + tool_latency_t + queueing_t + orchestration_t ],

with context_t growing every step. ChatGPT's framing sharpens this: the objective moves from token generation to **the critical path of a successful task**, and "behavioral equivalence, comparable benchmark accuracy, and an accuracy–cost tradeoff are different claims" [O]. Gemini's framing adds the serving-side view: agent sessions hold KV/tool/environment state through long idle waits, so retention and eviction policy become first-order costs [G].

## 2.2 Technique-by-technique mapping (merged from docs 1, 7, 9; cross-referenced to Part III)

| Inference technique | Agent-level analogue | Papers implementing it (provider tags) | Reported gains, with qualifications | Open problems (merged) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Speculative decoding** (draft k tokens, verify in one target pass; output distribution unchanged) | **Speculative actions / plans / observations**: a fast speculator predicts the next action(s) or tool result and pre-launches them while the authoritative actor deliberates; commit only on exact match; restrict to read-only/idempotent/sandboxed effects. Async function calling (futures) overlaps decode with tool execution | Speculative Actions (Columbia, ICLR 2026) [C,O,G]; Speculate with Memory (Salesforce, Jul 2026) [C,O,G]; Interactive Speculative Planning (ICLR 2025) [C,O]; Dynamic Speculative Agent Planning (ICLR 2026) [C,O,G]; PASTE (MSR/SJTU, Mar 2026) [C,O,G]; DualSpec (PKU/MSR, Mar 2026) [O,G]; Speculative Macro Commit (USC/Intel, Sep 2026) [C,O,G]; Spork (Jul 2026) [G]; AsyncLM (Yale, 2024) [C]; AsyncFC (Berkeley, May 2026) [O,G]; AOSpec, TClone, AAPT, SpecHop, IdleSpec, speculative tool calls (2512.15834) [C]; speculative-tools (GitHub) [G] | Speculative Actions: **19.5% less time** (chess, GPT-5) with 54.7% next-action prediction accuracy; "up to 55% / up to 20%" across gaming, e-commerce, web search; lossy OS extension. Speculate with Memory: **19–39% relative** action-prediction accuracy gain, **2.5×** observation-prediction gain; ALFWorld *estimated* latency reduction ~28%→>50%; HotpotQA 19,024 s baseline, 892 s saved (**1.05×** analytical). ISP: OpenAGI 182.70→105.42 s (**−42.30%**) but $0.2160→$0.2973; TravelPlanner soft matching drops commonsense micro-pass 48.6→41.7%. DSP: **37.09% latency** at **62.99% higher cost** vs sequential (offset 2); vs fixed-horizon speculation, 0.90× latency at 34.9% lower cost; "30% total cost, 60% unnecessary cost" vs fastest lossless. PASTE: v1 **48.5%** average task-completion-time reduction, 1.8× tool throughput; v3 average latency **up to 43.5%**, p99 **up to 55.4%**, 1.8× lower tool latency; tool-side 1.71× vs ORION, 1.83× vs SpecFaaS (v3 §6.3); v3 audit of >20,000 speculative actions: 602 potentially side-effecting blocked, no task with a different final result; Edit→Verify pattern 55%, Search→Visit 51% (v1). DualSpec: GAIA-Text 1,041→605 s (63.1→63.1%), XBench 1,007→480 s (66→66%); Gemini's "up to 3.28×" unverified. SMC: τ² Telecom 27.60→22.47 s (**−18.59%**, 99.52→99.52%); AppWorld 355.7→195.9 s (**−44.93%**, 41.67→40.48%); 3 GPUs vs 1; mined macros exposed as tools are rarely selected by Qwen3.5-27B. Spork: p95 **−18%** (Qwen3-32B). AsyncLM: **1.6–5.4×** (cloud latency emulated at 5 ms/token). AsyncFC: 1.24× (75.0→75.0%), 1.26× (54.8→53.2%), 1.12× (50→50%) | Speculating on **side-effecting** actions (rollback/transactions; Atomix epochs are one proposal); semantic vs exact-match verification with a correctness guarantee; bounding speculative cost under API pricing (extra branches are billed); GUI speculation with shared state, async updates and irreversible effects; drafters trained on the target agent's own trajectories |
| **KV / prefix caching — exact reuse** | **Cross-step KV retention across tool waits; program-aware eviction/prefetch; provider prompt caching; cache-friendly prompt layout** | KVFlow (NeurIPS 2025) [C,O]; Continuum (Berkeley/Stanford/Tsinghua; v7 Sep 2026) [C,O,G]; CacheBlend (EuroSys 2025 best paper) [C]; Preble (ICLR 2025) [O]; InferCept (ICML 2024) [O]; SGLang RadixAttention (NeurIPS 2024) [C,O,G]; CacheScout (Aug 2026) [G]; AAFLOW+ (Jul 2026) [C]; TokenPilot (Jun 2026) [G]; AsymCache, AgentKVShift, ConServe, DualPath, PBKV, UNISON [G]; "Don't Break the Cache" (Jan 2026) [C,G]; Browser Use history-before-state layout [C] | KVFlow: **1.83×** single / **2.19×** concurrent vs SGLang HiCache on synthetic large-prefix workloads; **1.08×** on realistic PEER Financial-QA. Continuum: **1.12–3.66× lower delay, 1.10–3.22× throughput** across three hardware/model setups (trace replay of GPT-5-collected SWE-Bench/BFCL/OpenHands traces, Poisson arrivals, Llama-3.1 8B/70B, Gemma-3 12B; §1 since v2); v4–v7 abstract ">8×" average JCT = "up to 8.18x" delay on a real SWE-agent run (500 SWE-Bench-Verified tasks, partner H100 testbed); RL-rollout microbenchmark 144.9 vs 93.4 (vLLM) vs 114.8 (ThunderAgent) steps/min, GLM-4.5-fp8, Multi-SWE-bench, 8×H100 (v6 Table 5; ≈1.55× vs vLLM, calc.). CacheBlend: TTFT 2.2–3.3×, throughput 2.8–5× (RAG, not agents). Preble: 1.5–14.5× mean request latency, 2–10× p99. SGLang: up to 6.4× throughput, 3.7× lower single-program latency. CacheScout: TTFT −18–45%, per-turn latency −29–38%, throughput +19–57%, hit rate +10–18 pp (single-source). TokenPilot: 61–87% serving-cost reduction (single-source). Provider caching: reads 0.1× (0.05×/0.025× on newest Claude), writes 1.25×/2×; OpenAI up to 80% TTFT / 90% cost; measured 41–80% cost and 13–31% TTFT on 500+ real sessions, with naive full-context caching sometimes *increasing* latency | Retention under unpredictable, heavy-tailed tool waits (needs tool-latency prediction; TraceLab lists it as an open opportunity); GPU-memory contention between pinned agent caches and new sessions; co-design with context compaction (every edit breaks the prefix; SmoothAgent quantifies the spikes); cross-model KV sharing in heterogeneous multi-agent systems; open-world UI noise that keeps invalidating prefixes |
| **KV / prefix caching — semantic reuse** (a higher-level analogue, not KV) | **Cache plans, workflows, executable routines or transition graphs across tasks; adapt parameters instead of regenerating** | Agentic Plan Caching (NeurIPS 2025) [C,O,G]; Agent Workflow Memory (ICML 2025) [C,O,G]; AgenticCache (MLSys 2026) [O]; Executable Agentic Memory (May 2026) [O]; PreAct (Jun 2026) [O]; Skim/Accio (May 2026) [C]; PEEK orientation cache (May 2026) [G]; MemRefine, SRMT [G] | APC: **−50.31% cost, −27.28% latency, 96.61%** of accuracy-optimal performance; GAIA/Open Deep Research **$69.02→$16.27 (−76.42%)** at 37.58→36.97% (−0.61 pp); 100-query FinanceBench latency 1,959→1,425 s. | **Continuous batching / iteration-level scheduling** | Two levels: **(i) dispatch independent tool calls from a dependency DAG; async futures; action batching per turn; (ii) schedule whole agent programs (attained service, dependencies, session locality) and sandboxes** | LLMCompiler (ICML 2024) [C,O,G]; Parrot (OSDI 2024) [C,O]; Autellix → Agentix (NSDI 2026) [C,O,G]; Continuum [C,O,G]; W&D parallel tool calling (Feb 2026) [O]; TPS-Bench (ACL 2026) [O]; SPACE action chunking (Sep 2026) [O,G]; UFO2 multi-action (TMLR 2026) [O,C]; OSWorld 2.0 batched tools [all]; SpecBox, DeltaBox (2026) [G]; Helium (Mar 2026) [G]; Pie (SOSP 2025) [O] | LLMCompiler: Movie Recommendation **20.47→5.47 s (3.74×)**, cost **6.73× lower** ($20.46→$3.04 per 1,000 tasks), accuracy 72.47→77.13% vs improved ReAct; ~35% faster than OpenAI parallel function calling; small regressions on other tasks. Pie: ~15% latency, 30% throughput | Non-clairvoyant schedulers cannot predict remaining program length (the analogue of output-length prediction); joint GPU + tool/sandbox scheduling under tool rate limits and tail latency; dependencies inside a single GUI (state) restrict parallelism, so fleet throughput does not shorten one task; stateless provider APIs hide program structure; dependency inference on non-deterministic tool outputs |
| **Distillation / draft models** | **Small action models** trained on teacher trajectories; per-step **routing/cascades** between small and large models; a strong model kept as verifier or fallback | FireAct (2023) [C,O]; Fara-7B (Microsoft, Nov 2025) [O]; Octopus v2 (2024) [C]; RouteLLM (ICLR 2025) [C,G]; FrugalGPT (2023) [C]; BoPO budget-aware routing (Cambridge/M365, Feb 2026) [O]; WebRouter (Oct 2025) [O]; StepWise selective escalation (Apr 2026) [O]; "SLMs are the Future of Agentic AI" (NVIDIA position) [C]; Self-Guide, VITA-VLA, m2mKD, SMART [G]; GoClick, GUI-G1 [O] | FireAct: prompted→fine-tuned GPT-3.5 **9.0→2.7 s/trial**, $0.0026→$0.0022, EM 31.4→39.2% (context shrinks when few-shot examples disappear); Llama-2-7B +77% HotpotQA. RouteLLM: up to 3.66× cost saving at 95% GPT-4 quality on MT-Bench (1.41× MMLU, 1.49× GSM8K; single-turn). BoPO: large-model calls 125%→88% of cap (≈29.6% fewer expensive calls), 66.7→66.5%. WebRouter: **$0.98→$0.12/task (−87.8%)**, 86.1→82.3%. StepWise (EvoCUA-8B + selective Sonnet 4.5 vs always-Sonnet, OSWorld): **$0.881→$0.224/task (−74.6%)**, 6.4→4.1 s per request, success 58.1→55.4% | Per-step routing where one cheap bad step compounds; confidence calibrated to eventual task success (silent semantic drift); distillation targets that preserve recovery behavior, not just happy paths; out-of-distribution reliability; amortized training and fallback overhead; small models' "tool overuse" loops |
| **Early exit / adaptive computation** | Three forms: **(a) selective reasoning** — spend expensive reasoning/verification only at uncertain transitions; **(b) eliminate determined decisions** — execute known sequences as deterministic macros/programs, skipping model calls, and re-enter reasoning on precondition failure; **(c) early termination of the loop** | StepWise [O]; AWO / meta-tools (Jan 2026) [O]; Ares adaptive reasoning effort (Mar 2026) [O]; Think Twice Click Once (2025) [O]; TAB adaptive thinking budgets (CMU, Apr 2026) [O]; EET early termination (Findings ACL 2026) [O]; "Runaway is Ashamed" (May 2025) [C]; Compiled AI (Apr 2026, industry) [C]; Agent JIT Compilation [C,O]; SkillDroid, PANDO [G]; Efficient Agents [all]; Dynamic tool gating [G]; Anthropic medium-vs-high effort [O] | AWO (GPT-5.1/AppWorld): **21.8→19.2 calls, 266.6→200.8 s (−24.7%)**, 69.6→73.2%; **counterexample with Claude 4.5: calls 16.7→15.4 but time rises 75.6→88.1 s and success declines 89.3→85.7%** (AWO v1 Table 9) → fewer calls ≠ faster. TAB: up to 20% fewer tokens, 15% lower latency (paired accuracy NR). EET: SWE-bench Verified $13.77→$6.18 (−55.1%), resolved 33.2→41.0% (one config loses 0.2 pp). Runaway: 50–70% fewer redundant steps; ALFWorld 19.0→13.4 steps at 76.1→70.2%. Compiled AI: 96% completion at zero execution tokens (BFCL n=400), break-even ~17 transactions, 57× fewer tokens at 1,000; 4.5 ms vs 2,004 ms median. Agent JIT: 150.1→15.4 s (9.7×), 61→90%. | **Quantization** (weakest analogy; literal weight/KV quantization still applies inside the model) | **Lossy compression of what is re-read every step**: observation masking, trajectory pruning, DOM/AXTree trimming, visual-token reduction, schema/tool-definition minimization, episodic key-fact extraction. **v2 additions to the mapping (from doc 15 [C15]; details and numbers in Part III):**
- *Speculative decoding →* SPORK (self-speculative KV forking, read-only tools; GAIA p95 −18%), Speculative Actions' proof of a 50% single-step cap, the LLNL/UMD engine-resident speculative tool calls (2512.15834).
- *KV / prefix caching, exact →* KVCOMM (cross-agent KV reuse via anchor offset correction, up to 7.8×; abstract claims no quality degradation, body reports <2.5% accuracy drop at 95% reuse on GSM8K) and DroidSpeak (KV sharing across fine-tuned variants, NSDI 2026, up to 4×; "negligible" F1/Rouge-L/code-similarity loss on non-agent long-context datasets) — both **approximate**, so they sit between exact reuse and lossy compression; InferCept's finding that KV recompute was 37–40% of forwarding time before interception-aware serving.
- *KV / prefix caching, semantic →* EchoPath (validated GUI trajectories as callable memories with preconditions and lifecycle states), AgentReuse (parameterized plan reuse, −93% latency on the SMP2019 Chinese assistant-command set of 2,664 requests; 93% reuse rate from 20 requests × 5 runs), ToolCaching (bandit-admission tool-result cache), AgentRR (record-and-replay proposal, no numbers).
- *Continuous batching →* LLM-Tool Compiler (fuses similar tool calls: −12% latency, −40% tokens), ParaGUI (planner–worker across desktop containers: ~50% fewer critical-path steps).
- *Distillation / draft models →* Fara-7B vs OpenAI computer-use-preview ($0.025 vs $0.913/task), Step-level Optimization's config range (58.2–59.3% vs 60.1%).
- *Early exit / adaptive computation →* API-first and hybrid API+GUI action spaces (AXIS ≈2× faster than a UI agent, −65–70% task time vs manual human work; ComputerRL +134% rel. success, "≤1/3 steps" unquantified; Beyond Browsing +24 pp) as the strongest form of "eliminate the decision"; The Danger of Overthinking (o1 low effort −43% cost, −1.8 pp), Budget Tracker/BATS (−31% cost at equal accuracy), TTI "Thinking vs Doing" (spend budget on actions, not per-step thinking; per-step budget forcing gave only ~3%).
- *Quantization →* Prune4Web (AAAI 2026; 25–50× fewer candidate elements, grounding 46.8→88.3%), ScreenSeekeR zoom-in as the counterexample that *adds* calls to buy accuracy (18.9→48.1%).

## 2.3 The cost-unit shift: from tokens to dependent decisions

Points that at least two providers made, with the strongest evidence for each:

1. **One environment step can contain several expensive model calls.** GTA1: 4–12 planning calls per judging call plus grounding; S2: planning + reflection + retrieval per step (E22, E25). TheAgentCompany counts LLM calls as steps by definition (E10). Consequently action count, model-call count and *sequential depth* must be measured separately [O, C].
2. **Production requests expand into mostly sequential inference chains.** Copilot: 6.6 model calls per user turn, concurrency 1.15 (E20). Claude Code/Codex: 8.8 LLM calls, 10.8 tool calls, 4.3 min per request (E19). So repeated request entry and dependency depth, not decode speed, set task latency [O, C].
3. **Growing context creates repeated logical input, but not necessarily repeated prefill.** OSWorld-Human's 3× later-step slowdown (uncached screenshot history) vs 84.6–99.5% cache hits and 91–98.6% decode share when KV state stays resident (E23, E29). "Agents are prefill-dominated" is not universal [O]. TraceLab's 95.7% hit rate still leaves prefix tokens as 59.5% of cost because cache misses cause 3.8× more tokens to be prefilled than truly unique input tokens (TraceLab §4.3) [C].
4. **Token prices are collapsing faster than task costs.** GPT-6 Luna at $0.10/$0.50 per M (Sept 2026) and DeepSeek V4 Flash at $0.09/$0.18 [V] make "saving a few thousand tokens" less valuable than "saving seconds of idle wait"; Gartner expects inference cost per agentic workflow to *rise* more than fivefold through 2028 (E55, 17 Aug 2026) and overall inference cost to rise (E62) [V].
5. **Tool waits dominate in some workloads and are heavy-tailed** (4% of tool calls = 85% of tool time; tool execution 35–61% of request time) [C, G] — the strongest argument for serving-side KV retention and speculative tool execution.
6. **Turns are the deployment-relevant cost** for interactive use (OSWorld 2.0) [V] — the strongest argument for compiled recipes and action batching.

## 2.4 Context-growth accounting (derived independently by ChatGPT and Claude)

For N model calls with initial context H₀ and Δ new tokens per call, the submitted input volume is Σᵢ Hᵢ = N·H₀ + Δ·N(N−1)/2 with Hᵢ = H₀ + (i−1)Δ. Worked examples: 10K-token prefix over 50 steps = 500K prefix tokens alone [C]; 5K start + 500/call over 100 calls = 2.975M input tokens, average 29,750 per call [O]; Gemini's budget assumes 5K→45K linear growth, average 25K. Caching changes the constant, not the shape (reads 0.1× or less; TTFT −13–31% measured); context editing restores recomputation (SmoothAgent). For serial workflows task time accumulates queueing + prefill + decode + tools + orchestration across calls; for parallel execution the dependency graph's **critical path** governs completion, so summing overlapping call durations overstates end-to-end time [O]. Dollar accounting must keep uncached-input, cached-input, output/reasoning and tool charges separate.

## 2.5 Single-slide thesis (the three providers' versions, then a merged one)

- **ChatGPT:** Agent acceleration is inference optimization lifted to a stateful execution graph — speculate transitions, retain reusable computation, compile familiar routines, schedule independent work, allocate model capacity by decision difficulty; the objective is lower wall-clock and dollars per successful task at a specified reliability level; papers validate mechanisms separately and PASTE shows why integration matters (accelerating tools alone creates returning-request congestion).
- **Claude:** In agentic workloads the unit of cost is the step, not the token; each call re-prefills a growing context interleaved with heavy-tailed tool latency; every classic inference optimization has a step-level twin; the largest verified gains (2–15× serving throughput, 30–70% fewer steps or cost) come from treating the agent *program* as the unit to schedule, cache and prune; the next frontier is step-level accounting plus verifiers that know when a cheaper step is safe.
- **Gemini:** The unit has shifted from tokens to stateful multi-turn steps; because agent workloads achieve 85–99% KV-cache hit rates while spending up to ~60% of their lifecycle waiting on sandboxes and tools, prefill-focused acceleration has diminishing returns; treat the LLM as the CPU of a distributed system and map micro-level techniques to the macro level.
- **Claude (P3 survey, doc 15):** The quickest way to make web, GUI and computer-use agents faster and cheaper is to cut the number and size of LLM calls, not to speed up each call; the methods that reduce calls (executable skills, site-derived tools, native APIs) lose the least accuracy and usually raise it; serving-layer KV reuse and provider caching are free; speculation is lossless but capped near 50% per step and untested on irreversible GUI clicks; the field's main weakness is measurement on incompatible benchmarks. Practitioner order: lossless layers first, call-count reducers second, lossy levers last and per-step.
- **Merged (recommended):** *Agents are programs, not prompts. Their cost is a critical path of dependent model calls over a growing context, interleaved with heavy-tailed tool waits; which term dominates depends on the observation modality and caching (three regimes). Every inference optimization has a step-level twin — speculate transitions, retain KV and reuse plans, batch and schedule at program level, route steps to small models, compile determined steps away, compress what is re-read — and the largest verified wins come from removing sequential decisions and environment turns, not from faster tokens. The levers stack in a definite order: lossless serving, caching and parallelism first (free), call-count reducers second (usually accuracy-positive), lossy per-step levers last. The research objective is wall-clock and dollars per successful task at a stated reliability, measured with calls, turns, tokens and tool time reported together.*

# PART III — Prompt 3: Research landscape 2023–2026, eight technique families

Merged from ChatGPT's two survey runs (docs 2, 3), Gemini's survey (doc 13), and relevant entries from the P2/P4/P5 reports. Labels follow ChatGPT's scheme: **S** = structurally/conditionally semantics-preserving; **E** = measured aggregate quality preserved or improved (not a losslessness proof); **L** = observed quality decline at the cited setting; **NR** = numerical comparison not reported/verified; **N/A** = benchmark or metric, not an accelerator. Numbers are the papers' table values or reported results unless marked calc.; "arXiv" means no refereed venue was verified.

## 3.2 Cross-task plan, workflow and trajectory caching

Plan reuse is approximate semantic reuse; prefix caching (§3.6) reuses exact model computation. Their correctness conditions differ [O].

## 3.3 Speculation: speculative actions and planning

Effect classes: reversible = run eagerly and compensate on abort; bufferable = defer with a staged ack; irreversible-gated = fire only on commit. Speculative branches follow the same rules: commit one, abort the rest. No snapshots; needs an adapter per tool | Irreversible-effect leaks over 500 invalid-send trials to a real SMTP/webhook sink: **Tx-Full 0/500 vs Saga-compensation 400/500 (80%), checkpoint-replay 200/500 (40%)**; a misclassified effect class leaks 300/500. Clean task success at fault prob. 0.30: 57% vs 0–7% for baselines (τ-bench retail; WebArena/OSWorld sweeps in App. C.1, not extracted). Wrong adapter metadata: 66/200 invariant violations (scopes too narrow), 17/200 leaks (wrong class). Overhead microseconds per call [E113–E116] | S mechanism + fault-injection evaluation; not a speed-up paper | G10, G11; verified 2026-09-27 (E113–E116) |

Speculative actions run in copy-on-write **filesystem forks**. A fork is committed only if both the action and its origin state match (JASV). "Effects that cannot be isolated remain ineligible"; non-filesystem effects are excluded | Terminal-Bench (4 harnesses, 5 actors): mean end-to-end latency −11.8–32.5%, p99 up to −42.8%. Observation-model exact hit rate 33.3% with 27.1% of tool time hidden (MSA × Sonnet 4.5 ablation). Action trace-matched accuracy 11.0 → 23.7% as width goes 1 → 32. End-to-end reuse fraction and extra compute not reported [E123] | S conditional (exact verification + fork isolation); coding/terminal, not web | C6, G9; verified 2026-09-27 |

A branch fires only after three suppression checks (eligible id, confidence threshold, deadline); late branches are "suppressed rather than executed", "producing no incorrect actions". There is no special handling of irreversible actions; the scope is limited to enumerable low-risk transitions. 0.50 → 0.79 success in a 650 ms decision window on the controlled key_prompt benchmark; DynaCU transfer is a tie (7/39 vs 6/39); no hit rate reported; arXiv Jul 2026 in AAAI-27 format, **not accepted as of 2026-09-27** (AAAI-27 decisions due 30 Nov 2026) [C15; E134]. n-gram tool predictors. 2512.15834 (LLNL/UMD inferred): engine-resident speculative tool calls reporting "several hundred tokens per second" throughput gains [C15] | Mostly unverified numbers (AAPT verified) | NR | C6, C5, G9, G10, C15 ⚠ |

Admissible only if the action is side-effect-free, idempotent, or staged behind a commit barrier; irreversible actions "must not be speculated, regardless of EV" | **No empirical results**; synthetic validation only (App. D). Lists capacity contention and tail-latency effects as unmodelled [E131] | Theory | C15 ⚠ → verified 2026-09-27 (D109) |

External effects are **staged in an outbox** with idempotency keys and released only after validation/approval (commit semantics). Lineage graph and recovery log. **No compensation of released external effects**. Operations that cross "external or opaque boundaries" are outside its containment scope | 45 author-built risky workflows (coding, incident response, docs, office, support, data; **no web/GUI**): Cordon intercepts 45/45 before commit vs 14 prevented / 26 missed / 5 post-commit for existing defences. Mean time 25.55 s (plain) → 31.35 s (approve-all), including human-approval wait (+22.7%, calc.); tokens −23.6–28.4%. Local rollback median 4.17 ms, recovery 178.95 ms, resume 15/15. τ-bench 87.5 → 90.0% [E117–E120] | S mechanism (commit barrier, not compensation) | G9; verified 2026-09-27 (D105) — relevant to Concur only where the UI offers a staging area (inference) |
| [ToolSpec: Accelerating Tool Calling via Schema-Aware …](https://arxiv.org/html/2604.13519v2) — arXiv Apr 2026 | Schema-aware speculative decoding of tool-call arguments | Numbers not extracted | NR | G9 ⚠ |
| [Ghost Tool Calls: Issue-Time Privacy for Speculative Agent Tools](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.02483) — Mohammadi, Klein, Arora, Bindschaedler; MPI-SWS / EPFL / Aarhus (Atomix group) — arXiv v1 1 Jun 2026 | Abandoned speculative calls ("ghost calls") are visible to providers, logs and caches **at issue time**, so no commit-time policy can undo them. Speculative Tool Privacy Contracts decide per event: allow / rewrite / shadow / defer / block | Median 8 speculative candidates per task, 82–89% abandoned. Intent recovery 0.97 (naive) vs 0.44 (no speculation); a **read-only restriction leaks as much as naive** (0.97; AgentDojo 0.750 vs 0.750); Rewrite 0.53, Shadow 0.42. Provider calls 4.03 vs 1.03 per task (≈3.9×, calc.). Async overlap saves 5.4% / 12.3% at p50 (single-tool / multi-tool); task success CIs overlap [E129, E130] | S (privacy), not a speed-up claim | New (§8.2 P2 item 16 lead); verified 2026-09-27 |
| [WebOperator: Action-Aware Tree Search for Autonomous Agents in Web Environment](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.12692) — Dihan, Hashem, Ali, Parvez; BUET / Monash / QCRI — arXiv v1 14 Dec 2025 (venue unconfirmed) | Web actions classified as safe / destructive / terminating / invalid. Pre-execution: buttons are potentially destructive, links safe. Post-execution: GET safe, POST/PUT/DELETE/PATCH destructive. A destructive action marks a "point of no return" and prunes the tree. Backtracking replays from a URL checkpoint in a parallel tab and is validated before use | WebArena 54.6% (gpt-4o). **Only ≈37% of pre-flagged actions were truly destructive**; ≈40% of successful tasks backtracked at least once. No latency-speculation hit rate [E133] | Accuracy method (tree search) | New; verified 2026-09-27 — the only published web-UI reversibility classifier found (D104) |
| [Revisable by Design](https://arxiv.org/html/2604.23283v1) — Zhai, Li, Wang; Fudan / Guangming Lab — arXiv v1 25 Apr 2026 | Idempotent / Reversible / Compensable / Irreversible taxonomy; earliest-conflict rollback keeps the I/R prefix, compensates the earliest conflicting K action and re-plans the tail | 14.6× fewer wasted steps than full restart (0.78 vs 11.41) at equal quality (3.07 vs 3.17) on StreamBench, n=1,008, **simulated tools** [E135] | Theory + simulated evaluation | New; verified 2026-09-27 |
| [Safe to Resume? Breaking Execution Continuity of Agent Execution via Rollback](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.29381) — Wu, Li, Jiang, Niu, Wang, Zhang; SUSTech / CityU HK — arXiv v1 29 Aug 2026 | Security study of checkpoint/restore in LangGraph, CrewAI, Hermes, Cline and E2B; five failure modes, including external effects that rollback erases from the agent's record | Both commit orderings fail on 90/96 and 93/96 microbenchmark workflows; unrecorded external effects in 40/1,735 executions (2.3%); duplicate-payment attack end to end [E136] | Security negative result | New; verified 2026-09-27 |
| [Robust Agent Compensation (RAC)](https://arxiv.org/html/2605.03409) — Perera, Hapuarachchi, Leymann, Khalaf; WSO2 / Stuttgart — arXiv v2 May 2026 (ACM CAIS '26 header) | LIFO compensation from a transaction log; compensations come from framework config, then MCP definitions, then an LLM prompt. **Fails open**: a tool with no compensation found is assumed side-effect-free | Numbers not used (metric definitions unclear) [E137] | Mechanism | New; partially verified |

**Isolation assumptions and live-portal feasibility (added in v3; E113–E138).** "Feasible without snapshots" asks whether the mechanism still works when the server-side state of a proprietary SaaS portal cannot be copied, forked or rolled back. The Concur-specific reading is an inference and has not been tested.

| Mechanism family | Papers | What it assumes | Feasible on a live SaaS portal without snapshots? | Reported hit / acceptance rate | Reported side-effect incidents |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fork / snapshot the environment | TClone, DeltaBox, AOSpec, SMC (draft state), Speculative Actions ("sandboxed") | Local state can be copied (container, CoW filesystem, CRIU); external connections are closed (TClone) or excluded (DeltaBox: "no network I/O rollback"; AOSpec: "non-filesystem … excluded") | **No** for server state. The local browser can be forked, but a Concur request from a fork reaches the real server | AOSpec obs. exact hit 33.3%; SMC fired 158/158 correct, commit rate 62.0–86.2%; TClone/DeltaBox none | None reported (absence) |
| Read-only / whitelist speculation | SPORK, Skim, Speculate with Memory, 2606.07846 (a) | The speculated call has no state effect | **Yes for state; no for observability or load.** Ghost Tool Calls: read-only leaks as much intent as naive speculation (0.97 vs 0.97), and naive speculation sends ≈3.9× the provider calls | SPORK α≈0.37 per turn; Speculative Actions 22–46% API-call prediction | None reported; leakage measured (E129) |
| Staging / commit barrier | Cordon (outbox), Atomix (bufferable, irreversible-gated), 2606.07846 (c), PASTE (dry-run / staging) | The effect can be held and released later: an outbox the runtime owns, or a staging resource/API | **Only where the portal itself offers a staging state** (e.g. an unsaved form, or a saved but unsubmitted draft; inference). A GUI click that POSTs cannot be held by the agent runtime without an interposing proxy | PASTE 93.8% "overall hit rate" (undefined) | Cordon 45/45 intercepted; Atomix Tx-Full 0/500 leaks; PASTE 602 blocked, 0 divergent results |
| Compensation after the fact | Atomix (reversible class), Speculative Actions (repair paths), Revisable by Design (K class), RAC | A semantic inverse exists and a durable effect log survives rollback | **Partially**: only for compensable UI actions (e.g. delete a draft line; inference). Never for irreversible ones (submit, approve, payment) | — | Saga-compensation leaked 400/500 (Atomix); commit ordering fails 90–93/96 (Safe to Resume); RAC fails open |
| Classification before acting (gate) | WebOperator, AAPT, EchoPath (start-state gate), SMC (API-name rules) | A predicate over the action or state decides whether it may run early or replay | **Yes, and needed by every row above**, but reported precision is low: WebOperator pre-execution flag 37% precise; EchoPath gate falsely accepts 22% of incompatible starts (E92) | — | None reported |

Lossless speculation requires valid isolation, matching, commit and recovery semantics; adding a verifier alone does not establish it [O]. Speculation also costs dollars: ISP nearly doubled per-task cost with a ReAct target [C]. Three 2026 results bound the design space (E113, E129, E136). First, gating before release beats compensation after the fact: Atomix's Saga-compensation baseline leaked 80% of irreversible effects against 0% for gated commit. Second, rollback that restores only the agent's side can erase the record of an external effect, which enables a duplicate payment. Third, 'read-only' speculation is not invisible: abandoned speculative calls reveal intent to the provider at issue time, and a read-only restriction leaks as much as unrestricted speculation. No paper in this table evaluates speculation against a live, proprietary SaaS back end.

| [W&D: Scaling Parallel Tool Calling for Deep Research](https://arxiv.org/html/2602.07359v1) — Lin, Liew, Savarese, Li — arXiv Feb 2026 | Increase native tool-call width per turn while controlling depth | BrowseComp first 100 (GPT-5-medium), 1→3 tools/turn: **1,522.6→904.2 s (−40.6%)**, $102.5→$65.7 (−35.9%), 66→68% | E (independent search calls parallelize; dependent GUI actions usually do not) | O3 ✅ |

Docker commit, Btrfs/LVM, Firecracker snapshot, CRIU, LangGraph Checkpointer | Task quality NR; code **not public** (placeholder repo github.com/dongyunpeng-sjtu/deltabox, "plan to release the code after publication") | G9 ✅ verified 2026-09-27 (followup-5, E177); "restore 900→1.86 ms" not in v1 (D126) |

| [ParaGUI / ParaGUIBench](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2607.22689) — arXiv Jul 2026 (authors unverified; alphaXiv source) | Planner–worker system; workers run in **separate desktop containers** | **~50% fewer critical-path steps; under half the tokens** | 46.4% vs 33.5% for a serial Claude Sonnet 4.6 baseline | C15 ⚠ — the only GUI parallelism result that spreads work across desktops |

Two notes from doc 15 [C15]: OSWorld-Human's human **grouped-action** trajectories need 4.5 steps vs 13.2 single-action steps on LibreOffice Calc, which bounds the gain from emitting several GUI actions per planning call; and acting without re-observing the screen risks acting on stale state — the same irreversibility problem speculation faces. Doc 15 also states that "no controlled study" measures how success changes when a computer-use agent emits k>1 GUI actions per call on OSWorld; that is **overstated** — UFO2's multi-action ablation on OSWorld-W (6.80→3.30 steps, 24.5→26.5%) and OSWorld 2.0's batched-vs-single comparison (Opus 4.8 20.6% vs 18.5%; Opus 4.7 18.2% vs 13.9%) [V] are exactly such comparisons (D46).

Grounding accuracy is not complete-task success; token pruning must be evaluated against small-text and coordinate errors; exact prompt caching (§3.6) must not be confused with dropping visual evidence [O]. Doc 15's ranking of this family by cost-cutting power: specialized small models (≈10–36×) > prompt caching (2–5× on input, lossless) > observation pruning (≈2× fewer tokens, small losses) > visual-token pruning (1.4–3.4× compute) [C15].

Within-program KV hit rate >90% (§3, Fig. 7) | S mechanism; accuracy NR; repeats NR; no code link in the paper | O2, O3, C7, G9, C15 ✅ verified 2026-09-27 (research/2026-09-27-followup-5-agent-serving-2026.md, E185) |

| [KVCOMM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12872) — Ye et al.; Duke, MIT et al. — NeurIPS 2025 poster (arXiv v2 1 Nov 2025) | Reuses KV for shared text under **different prefixes across agents** via anchor-based offset correction | >70% reuse; **up to 7.8× speedup; TTFT ~430→~55 ms** (five fully-connected agents, 1K input tokens with 512 prefix and 512 output tokens) | **Approximate** — abstract: "all without quality degradation" on RAG, math reasoning and collaborative coding (MMLU, GSM8K, HumanEval; Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct); body: "less than 2.5% accuracy drop" at 95% reuse, 4-agent GSM8K, 1,319 samples. Not agent tasks | C15 ✅ verified 2026-09-27 (research/2026-09-27-verification-P0-P1.md) |

Sydney — arXiv v1 11 Mar 2026 | Algorithm–system co-design for agent serving on one consumer GPU | **Up to 2.8× TTFT and 2.7× TPOT** vs SGLang, vLLM, llama.cpp; RTX A5000 or RTX 5090; Qwen2.5-3B/7B, LLaMA-3-8B; ToolBench (ReAct, Plan-and-Execute) | S; quality NR; code not found | ✅ verified 2026-09-27 (followup-5, E183) |

Continuum v6/v7 Table 5 reports the reverse ordering in an RL-rollout microbenchmark (D129) | S; quality NR; code https://github.com/ThunderAgent-org/ThunderAgent | New lead from P0-P1 ✅ verified 2026-09-27 (followup-5, E186) |
| ConServe (2606.01839); Workflow-Aware Prefix-State Scheduling (2608.25523); Stateful Inference for multi-agent tool calling (2605.26289) | PD-disaggregated pinning; prefix-state scheduling; stateful multi-agent inference | Not verified | NR | G11–G13 ⚠ |
| [AAFLOW+](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.10987) — arXiv Jul 2026 | Stateful operator abstraction with zero-copy distributed KV orchestration | 7.60× vs SGLang prefix caching at 16 agents (snippet only) | NR | C7 ⚠ |
| [SmoothAgent](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.00151v1) — arXiv Jul 2026 | Studies latency spikes when context transformations invalidate cached prefixes | Mechanism/measurement | N/A | O1 ✅ |
| Provider docs: [Anthropic prompt caching](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching), [OpenAI Prompt Caching 201](https://developers.openai.com/cookbook/examples/prompt_caching_201), Anthropic mid-conversation system messages (Opus 4.8) and tool changes (docs) | See E83, E84 | — | — | C7, C5, G12 |
| [TraceLab dashboard](https://tracelab.cs.washington.edu) — UW — live dashboard, data 23 Sep 2025 – 24 Jul 2026 (accessed 2026-09-27) | Counterfactual: keep the prefix cache across human think time for user-initiated steps | **"Final cost saved 15.8%"** (upper bound; append reduction 2.70B tokens, $14,333) over 665,453 steps / 8,058 sessions / 52 users of Claude Code and Codex | N/A (counterfactual estimate, not a deployed system) | New lead from P0-P1; extends E19 (followup-5, E187) — the only real-trace bound on what TTL-style retention (Continuum) buys when the idle gap is human time |

Scheduling and exact reuse preserve the intended computation, but absence of a quality evaluation must not be reported as measured zero loss; loaded-server gains differ from isolated-user latency gains [O]. There is an inherent conflict between prefix residency for idle agents and global batch size for new users [G]. **None of these serving systems was evaluated on a GUI/computer-use workload.** Re-checked 2026-09-27 against the primary papers for all 15 systems in followup-5: the workloads are SWE-bench, BFCL, GAIA, ShareGPT, LATS/HotpotQA, ToolBench, MCPBench, LoCoMo, long-context QA, workflow primitives and production RL traces, and none uses a browser, OSWorld or WebArena. Only 3 of 15 report a task-quality delta (TokenPilot, PEEK, AgentKVShift); 6 release code; headline numbers use different baselines and hardware and must not be compared directly. Quality risk is concentrated in the *approximate* sharers (KVCOMM, DroidSpeak, GUI-KV, AgentKVShift), not in exact reuse.

| [TraceLab](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.30560v2) — Zhu et al.; UW — Jun 2026 (v2, 30 Jun 2026) | 4,265 sessions; cache-miss prefill (3.8× unique tokens); tool-latency tails (E19) | Workload characterization; authors' own usage traces; verified 2026-09-27 | C7, G13 |
| [Agentic AI Workload Characteristics](https://arxiv.org/html/2605.26297v1) — Yuan, Nayak, Kundu, Talati (May 2026); AgentRace: Benchmarking Efficiency in LLM Agent Frameworks (anonymous OpenReview submission; a different paper) | Cache-hit and decode-share profiles (E29); framework efficiency benchmarking (AgentRace) | Workload characterization | O1, G9 |
| [When Web Agents Finish but Still Fail](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.20724v2) (Jun 2026); [OSWorld-Pro](https://arxiv.org/html/2609.24890v1) (Sep 2026); WebArena-Verified (258-task Hard subset); MyPCBench (2026); Agents' Last Exam (2026); [DolphinBench](https://arxiv.org/html/2609.24971) (Sep 2026); [Multi-Dimensional Framework for Enterprise Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/html/2511.14136v1) (2025) | Process-based and verified evaluation variants | Candidate testbeds; not yet verified in detail | G10, G11, G13, OSWorld 2.0 [V] |
| [Survey on Evaluation of LLM-based Agents](https://aclanthology.org/2026.findings-acl.1330/) — Yehudai et al.; IBM/Yale — Findings ACL 2026 (arXiv 2503.16416) | Evaluation methods, benchmarks and gaps incl. cost-efficiency | Reference | O3, G13 |

A useful evaluation bundle (merged): success with uncertainty; all-attempt monetary/compute cost; complete-task median and tail latency including failures; failure/retry costs; model calls, tokens and tool time; cache-hit rate; warm vs cold; pass^k; cost-of-pass computed at the problem level (a ratio of aggregate means hides heterogeneous failure probabilities) [O].

## 3.9 What remains open (merged across the three surveys)

1. **Safe abstraction and invalidation.** When does a compiled skill/recipe/cached plan still apply after UI, data or permission changes? Precondition validation, drift detection and safe return to observation are unsolved; today's fallback is reactive failure [O, G].
2. **Full lifecycle economics.** Demonstrations, exploration, compilation, training, warm-up, verification and repair are real costs; reuse pays only after amortization (PreAct's +162–217% verify overhead; SpeedRunner's growing library cost; break-even estimates like Compiled AI's ~17 transactions) [O, C].
3. **Joint control.** Model choice, reasoning effort, screenshot detail, action-chunk length and speculation depth interact; savings can cancel (pruning breaks caches; routers/verifiers eat latency; speculation raises API cost). Individual speedups must not be multiplied [O, C].
4. **Real GUI speculation.** Shared state, rollback, asynchronous updates and irreversible effects limit which branches can be pre-executed; snapshot/fork substrates (TClone, DeltaBox, SMC) do not exist for live SaaS [O, C, G].
5. **End-to-end evidence and non-inferiority.** Held-out long workflows, paired outcomes, uncertainty, deadlines and recovery; grounding scores and successful-only latency are insufficient; equal point estimates are weak evidence [O].
6. **Serving–agent co-design.** Expose dependencies and likely tool-wait times to runtimes; account for tool rate limits, multi-user load, tail latency, and the residency-vs-batch-size conflict [O, G].
7. **Macro-action boundary detection and credit assignment**; mid-chunk interruption signals; scaling hybrid GUI+CLI wrappers across apps [G].
8. **Difficulty estimation before attempting the task** (chicken-and-egg for budget routing); high-variance RL rewards over long near-deterministic sequences [G].
9. **Observation completeness vs cost** — pruned pages lose context needed for recovery ("semantic error within a state"); observation freshness vs call frequency [G].
10. **Standard accounting unit** — none of the works proposes a standard "model calls per solved task + prefill tokens per task + turns + tool time" unit; this is a gap a paper can fill [C]. Doc 15 adds that no study compares families (skills vs APIs vs speculation vs routing) on one benchmark with shared cost accounting; WES and cost-of-pass are the leading candidates for a standard [C15].
11. **Replay under drift and cache poisoning** — skills, caches and compiled workflows run deterministically, so stale or wrong entries act without LLM oversight; EchoPath's lifecycle management (promote / repair / quarantine) is the most developed design, but it is described, not evaluated, and EchoPath's only perturbation is a display-resolution shift (1920×1080→1600×900); its authors state it does "not yet establish live robustness under broad interface drift" — **no work measures replay success across UI versions over time**; poisoning of shared caches is unstudied while key-collision attacks on semantic caches already exist ([arXiv 2601.23088](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.23088v1)) [C15].
12. **Joint thinking- and action-budget control** — research treats the reasoning budget (Ares, TAB, Overthinking) and the action budget (BATS, TTI) separately; learned effort routers have only been shown on small open models; vendor effort knobs lack same-model ablations on agent benchmarks [C15].
13. **Whole-stack measurement** — no paper has measured the full stack (lossless serving + call-count reducers + lossy per-step levers) end to end on a computer-use benchmark with dollars and wall-clock; "whoever does will likely set the field's next reference point" [C15] — this is the opening the Part V plan can take.

- Preprint vs publication dates are distinguished where found; a manuscript template does not imply acceptance (Agent JIT is ICML-formatted but unverified) [O, C].
- Agentix's NSDI numbers replace the older Autellix numbers; Continuum's ">8×" (abstract since v4, still in v7) is a real-SWE-agent best case, and its 1.12–3.66× range is the controlled trace-replay result (D61); FocusAgent's Aug-2026 (v2/TMLR) main table replaces v1's (baselines revised 53.0→53.6% and 41.8→44.5%; GPT-5-mini retriever rows added), and every FocusAgent number must name its retriever (D15, D48); TPS-Bench's ACL table replaces its abstract; PASTE v3 replaces v1 [O].
- Log2Plan's time/success transposition and OS-Catalyst's timing configuration are unresolved [O].
- Gemini's landscape contains one non-existent paper (MacroPO), two mis-stated results (APC, Speculate with Memory), several entries sourced from an awesome-list, a personal homepage, a YouTube video and a Web3 blog, and two off-topic robotics papers (SEAR, DREAM-Chunk). Its unique, plausible additions (SkillDroid, PANDO, MFS/GEPA, Prune4Web, SWE-Pruner, Qwen-UI-Agent, CUA-Universe, CacheScout, Helium, TokenPilot, PEEK, SpecBox, DeltaBox, AsymCache, AgentKVShift, Atomix, Spork, Cordon, ToolSpec, AutoTool) should be verified before citation.
- Further Gemini-cited leads not yet examined: [Evaluating Temporal Semantic Caching and Workflow Optimization](https://arxiv.org/html/2605.20630v1) (cache invalidation under temporal drift — relevant to §3.9 item 1), [Learning to Curate Task-Adaptive Memory for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/html/2609.27334v1), Scale's MCP Atlas and the Tool-Decathlon initiative (live-server tool evaluations), and Budget-Aware Value Tree Search (2603.12634).
- NR never means zero measured loss [O].
- **Doc 15 [C15] hygiene (added in v2).** Strengths: it resolves venue conflicts from venue pages (WALT = ICLR 2026 poster; AI Agents That Matter = TMLR 2025), flags its own automated extraction (Ares), separates "lossless by construction" from "reported better", and lists what it did not check. Weaknesses: it relies on FocusAgent v1 and AppAgentX v1 numbers (D44, D48); repeats Efficient Agents' abstract figures (D9); states no controlled multi-action study exists on OSWorld (D46); marks Autellix's venue unverified although NSDI 2026 (Agentix) exists (D52); and by its own account did not examine OSWorld 2.0, TheAgentCompany, Preble, CacheBlend, LMCache or the UI-TARS/OS-Atlas/Aguvis primaries. Its ⚠-unverified venue list (UFO2, ComputerRL, SkillWeaver, AppAgentX, EchoPath, ToolCaching, AgentRR, AWO, Speculative Actions, PASTE, SPORK, AsyncLM, LLM-Tool Compiler, SPACE, ParaGUI, Fara-7B, StepWise, GUIPruner, Don't Break the Cache, Autellix, Continuum, CacheScout, Overthinking, BATS, TTI, Ares, TAB, Efficient Agents, Agent JIT, Agentic Compilation) is a useful checklist; note that several of these have since been placed by other documents (UFO2 TMLR 2026; Autellix → Agentix NSDI 2026; SPACE "EMNLP 2026" per G13; SMC "MLSP 2026" per O3).
- **Leads doc 15 names but did not check (for the verification queue):** ProAgent / agentic process automation, WorkflowLLM, AutoManual, Synatra, AgentKB, TinyAgent, UI-TARS / OS-Atlas / Aguvis primary papers, LMCache, Gemini thinking-budget agent results, s1 / L1 / AdaptThink, the "Ghost Tool Calls" privacy paper, and semantic-cache key-collision attacks (2601.23088).
